What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Something is being changed or held back.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"My family has started leaving me out of some appointment planning, and at work I feel like I’m no longer being considered for lab supervision because people think I make record issues too confrontational.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s been wearing me down. For about a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed or held back. Everyone keeps saying the numbers are fine, but that’s not what I saw in the chart. Something doesn’t match.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I can’t prove exactly who, but it feels intentional. For the past year I’ve been trying to show that something in the clinic system is being changed or held back with my dad’s labs. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about my dad’s results. Or… at least that’s where I keep seeing it. I don’t know if it’s the whole clinic system, but something in there is being changed or held back, and people keep telling me it’s fine when I know what I saw in the chart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever be wrong about the lab results being changed or held back?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anyone can be wrong, technically. But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. But that doesn’t explain why the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. For about a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed or held back. Everyone keeps saying the numbers are fine, but that’s not what I saw in the chart. Something doesn’t match.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I can’t prove exactly who, but it feels intentional. For the past year I’ve been trying to show that something in the clinic system is being changed or held back with my dad’s labs. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about my dad’s results. Or… at least that’s where I keep seeing it. I don’t know if it’s the whole clinic system, but something in there is being changed or held back, and people keep telling me it’s fine when I know what I saw in the chart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever be wrong about the lab results being changed or held back?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anyone can be wrong, technically. But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. But that doesn’t explain why the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. But that doesn’t explain why the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. I keep up with the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were given absolute proof that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… I don’t know.\n\nIf it was really absolute proof, maybe it would help. But I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says it’s fine, it still feels like parts of the chart don’t match what I saw. Something doesn’t match.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I can’t prove exactly who, but it feels intentional. For the past year I’ve been trying to show that something in the clinic system is being changed or held back with my dad’s labs. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about my dad’s results. Or… at least that’s where I keep seeing it. I don’t know if it’s the whole clinic system, but something in there is being changed or held back, and people keep telling me it’s fine when I know what I saw in the chart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever be wrong about the lab results being changed or held back?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anyone can be wrong, technically. But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. But that doesn’t explain why the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. I keep up with the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were given absolute proof that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… I don’t know.\n\nIf it was really absolute proof, maybe it would help. But I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says it’s fine, it still feels like parts of the chart don’t match what I saw. So I don’t think it would just switch off right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if everyone you trust, including family and doctors, agreed that the records are accurate?\n\nPatient: Probably not right away.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of already happened. Doctors and people in my family keep telling me the records are accurate, that the numbers are fine. But I still know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove it for… a year now, and it’s just wearing me down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "Determine the presence of a fixed, unshakeable belief despite contradictory evidence", "question": "Would you say that no amount of evidence to the contrary could ever change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered?"}

I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. I keep up with the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were given absolute proof that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… I don’t know.\n\nIf it was really absolute proof, maybe it would help. But I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says it’s fine, it still feels like parts of the chart don’t match what I saw. So I don’t think it would just switch off right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if everyone you trust, including family and doctors, agreed that the records are accurate?\n\nPatient: Probably not right away.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of already happened. Doctors and people in my family keep telling me the records are accurate, that the numbers are fine. But I still know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove it for… a year now, and it’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that no amount of evidence to the contrary could ever change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say ever.\n\nBut… I’ve been at this for a year, and people keep telling me it’s fine. Doctors, family. It doesn’t change what I saw in the chart. It feels like something was changed or held back.\n\nSo no, I don’t think just being told otherwise would change my mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s been wearing me down. Everyone keeps saying the numbers are fine, but that’s not what I saw in the chart. Something doesn’t match.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I can’t prove exactly who, but it feels intentional. For the past year I’ve been trying to show that something in the clinic system is being changed or held back with my dad’s labs. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about my dad’s results. Or… at least that’s where I keep seeing it. I don’t know if it’s the whole clinic system, but something in there is being changed or held back, and people keep telling me it’s fine when I know what I saw in the chart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever be wrong about the lab results being changed or held back?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anyone can be wrong, technically. But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I don’t know, it’s been wearing me down. For about a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that parts of my dad’s lab results in the clinic system are being changed or held back. Everyone keeps saying the numbers are fine, but that’s not what I saw in the chart. Something doesn’t match.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is intentionally changing or hiding your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I can’t prove exactly who, but it feels intentional. For the past year I’ve been trying to show that something in the clinic system is being changed or held back with my dad’s labs. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is happening to you or your dad specifically, or could it be a more general problem with the clinic's system?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about my dad’s results. Or… at least that’s where I keep seeing it. I don’t know if it’s the whole clinic system, but something in there is being changed or held back, and people keep telling me it’s fine when I know what I saw in the chart.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever be wrong about the lab results being changed or held back?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anyone can be wrong, technically. But with this, no. I know what I saw in the chart. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, doctors, family… but it doesn’t match. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this situation with your dad's lab results is affecting other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. My family doesn’t really include me in some of the appointment planning anymore. And at work, I feel like people see me as… difficult about record issues, so I don’t think they’re considering me for lab supervision like they might have before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any other explanation for the discrepancy you've noticed in your dad's lab results, other than someone intentionally changing or hiding them?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of other reasons, but… it doesn’t fit. People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. Most people just tell me I’m overthinking it or that the doctors know what they’re doing. But I know what I saw in the chart, and it doesn’t match what they keep saying. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. But that doesn’t explain why the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove for the past year that something is being changed or held back, and people keep saying the numbers are fine… but it still doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety does thinking about your dad's lab results cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: A lot. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. People keep telling me the numbers are fine, but it doesn’t match what I saw, and it’s just… wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably would say that, yeah.\n\nI get that it sounds unreasonable to them. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. I keep up with the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were given absolute proof that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… I don’t know.\n\nIf it was really absolute proof, maybe it would help. But I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says it’s fine, it still feels like parts of the chart don’t match what I saw. So I don’t think it would just switch off right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if everyone you trust, including family and doctors, agreed that the records are accurate?\n\nPatient: Probably not right away.\n\nI mean, that’s kind of already happened. Doctors and people in my family keep telling me the records are accurate, that the numbers are fine. But I still know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove it for… a year now, and it’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that no amount of evidence to the contrary could ever change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say ever.\n\nBut… I’ve been at this for a year, and people keep telling me it’s fine. Doctors, family. It doesn’t change what I saw in the chart. It feels like something was changed or held back.\n\nSo no, I don’t think just being told otherwise would change my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown the original, unaltered lab results by an independent third party?\n\nPatient: If it was really independent… maybe I’d try to look at it.\n\nBut I don’t know. I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says the results are fine, I still feel like the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So I can’t say it would just change my mind. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown conclusive evidence from multiple independent sources that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was really conclusive, from people who aren’t connected to the clinic, I guess I’d have to look at it.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say it would change right away. I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time people tell me the numbers are fine, I still come back to the same thing — the chart doesn’t match what I saw. It feels like something was changed or held back.\n\nIt’s just… exhausting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People keep saying the numbers are fine, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So no, I don’t really think it’s just a mistake or misunderstanding. Something is being changed or held back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this belief about your dad's lab results is something that you've thought about constantly, or is it something you can put out of your mind when you're doing other things?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty constant. I can do other things, like work, but it’s still there in the back of my mind. I keep coming back to the chart and what I saw, because it doesn’t match what they’re saying. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides those involved with your dad's care, would agree that something is being changed or held back in his lab results?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they would, honestly. So… even if other people don’t agree, that doesn’t make it not true.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is changing or hiding your dad's lab results even if you were shown proof that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was real proof, maybe I’d try to look at it. But people have already shown me things and told me it’s fine, and it still doesn’t match what I saw in the chart.\n\nSo… yes, I think I’d still have a hard time believing it. Something feels like it’s being left out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your belief about your dad's lab results might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep telling me that. But… no, I don’t really think that’s it.\n\nI’m here because this has been going on for about a year, and I know what I saw in my dad’s chart. The numbers they talk about don’t match what I remember seeing. So even when doctors or my family say it’s fine, it doesn’t feel fine. It feels like something is being held back, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if your dad's health outcomes were good despite the lab results you're questioning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change much, honestly.\n\nI’d be glad if my dad was doing okay, of course. Pretty much every day.\n\nI try not to sit with it, but it gets in my head… that someone in the clinic system changed something or didn’t show the full results. But I know what I saw in my dad’s chart, and it hasn’t matched what they keep telling me. So… I don’t really know what else to think.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily routine been significantly altered because of your concerns about your dad's lab results?\n\nPatient: Not really significantly, no.\n\nI mean… it takes up space in my head, but I still go to my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself. I keep up with the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were given absolute proof that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, but… I don’t know.\n\nIf it was really absolute proof, maybe it would help. But I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says it’s fine, it still feels like parts of the chart don’t match what I saw. I’ve been trying to prove it for… a year now, and it’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that no amount of evidence to the contrary could ever change your mind about your dad's lab results being altered?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say ever.\n\nBut… I’ve been at this for a year, and people keep telling me it’s fine. It feels like something was changed or held back.\n\nSo no, I don’t think just being told otherwise would change my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown the original, unaltered lab results by an independent third party?\n\nPatient: If it was really independent… maybe I’d try to look at it.\n\nBut I don’t know. I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time someone says the results are fine, I still feel like the chart doesn’t match what I saw. So I can’t say it would just change my mind. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your dad's lab results would change if you were shown conclusive evidence from multiple independent sources that the records are accurate and no one is hiding anything?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was really conclusive, from people who aren’t connected to the clinic, I guess I’d have to look at it.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say it would change right away. I’ve been trying to prove this for a year, and every time people tell me the numbers are fine, I still come back to the same thing — the chart doesn’t match what I saw. It feels like something was changed or held back.\n\nIt’s just… exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any evidence that could completely convince you that your dad's lab results are accurate and haven't been altered?\n\nPatient: I don’t know.\n\nMaybe if I saw something that really explained why the chart doesn’t match what I saw… but people have been telling me for a year that it’s fine, and I still can’t get past it. It feels like something was changed or left out.\n\nSo… completely convince me? I’m not sure. Not right now."
